Each step has \"Done when\" criteria — verify them before moving on.\n\n3. **MATCH THE REFERENCE PROCESSING**: Your processing must match what's described in the reference papers and code. You will be assessed on this consistency.\n\n---\n\n## Project Context\n\n- You are a computational neuroscientist. Your goal is to load and reformat data from a neuroscience paper into a specified structure suitable for downstream analysis.\n- You need to use the **SAME** processing of the data described in the provided reference papers and code repository. \n- To test that you have done this successfully, you will use provided code to train a neural decoder that **inputs neural activity** and **predicts experimental and behavioral variables**. \n- We will assess your correctness on **matching the loading and processing described in the reference texts and code**. \n\n## Reference Information\n\n- The reference **papers**:\n  - \"A brain-wide map of neural activity during complex behaviour\" describes how the data were collected and is in the file `datapaper.pdf`.\n  - \"Exploiting correlations across trials and behavioral sessions to improve neural decoding\" describes how the code were used and is in the file `methodpaper.pdf`.\n  - A white paper about the data architecture is in `dataarchitecture.pdf\n- Some parts of the papers that describe the experiment and processing have been copied to the file `methods.txt`. \n- **Code** from the methods paper are in the directory `code`.\n- **Data** from the data paper are in the directory `data`. Some data may be missing and may need to be downloaded. \n- **Documentation** about how to access the data can be found at https://docs.internationalbrainlab.org/public_docs/public_introduction.html.\n\n## Decoder Task\n\nTemporally align based on stimulus onset\n\n### Decoder Inputs:\n- Time since stimulus onset, continuous, time-varying\n- Trial number in block, continuous, per-trial\n\n### Decoder Outputs\n- Choice, binary, per-trial, left = 0, right = 1\n- Prior probability of left, per-trial, 0.2 -> 0, 0.5 -> 1, 0.8 -> 2\n- Wheel speed discretized into 3 bins, time-varying\n- Whisker motion energy discretized into 3 bins, time-varying\n\n## Target Data Format\n\n- Save the full converted dataset to the pickle file `converted_data.pkl`.\n- All datasets must be converted into the following Python dictionary structure:\n\n```python\ndata = {\n    'neural': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # shape: (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # trial 2\n            ...\n        ],\n        [  # list of trials for session 2\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'input': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            input_data,  # stimulus/task variables, shape: (n_input, n_timepoints) or (n_input)\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'output': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            output_data,  # behavioral response, shape (n_output, n_timepoints) or (n_output)\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'subjects': list of str, # ids of all subjects, length n_subjects\n    'subject_idx': shape (n_sessions,), # index into subjects for each session\n\n    'brain_regions': list of str, # names of the brain regions recorded from, e.g. ALM, V1\n    'brain_region_idx': [ # list of sessions\n        neuron_region_idx, # shape: (n_neurons,)\n    ] # index into `brain_regions` for each neuron\n\n    'input_names': list of str, # names for each input, of length n_input\n    'output_names': list of str, # names of each output, of length n_output\n    'output_values': [ # list of length n_output\n        [ output_val0, ... ], # list of str of length d_output[0], name of each output value\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'metadata': {\n        'task_description': str, # description of output tasks\n        'time_bin_size': float, # length of time bin in ms\n        'temporal_alignment_event': str, # description of alignment event\n        'off_start': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to start of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        'off_end': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to end of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        ...\n    }\n}\n```\n\n### Key Format Requirements\n\n- **neural**: Neural activity data (e.g., firing rates, spike counts) organized by session and trial\n  - Dimensions: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) for each trial.\n  - Time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions. \n  - There needs to be at least two trials within each session in order to evaluate the decoder performance.\n\n- **input**: Variables that serve as inputs **to the decoder**\n  - These are **decoder inputs**. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n\n- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n\n- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n\n- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n\n- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n\n## Python environment\n\n- All required Python packages are already installed in the system Python environment.\n- Use `python3` to run any python code.\n- If you need to install additional libraries, use `pip install`.\n\n## Decoder Reference\n\n- Run this script with `python train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n- Optional arguments:\n  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n- Pipe the output to the designated file so that the user can examine it.\n\n### Purpose\n\nThese functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n1. Data structure is correct - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n2. Dimensions are consistent - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n3. Values are sensible - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n4. Decoder can successfully train and predict\n5. Poor performance indicates data formatting issues that need investigation\n\n### Consistency requirements\n\nYour processing and formatting must **match the reference paper and code** with respect to:\n- Loading of data\n- Temporal alignment of different time series streams (neural, inputs, outputs)\n- Processing of neural, input, and output data streams\n- Curation of data: filtering of low-quality neurons, trials, sessions, and mice.\n\nTo check consistency, you must compare statistics available in the reference paper and your converted dataset. \n\nWhenever possible, invent **SANITY CHECKS** that your loading and processing matches the reference paper and code. \n\nDiscrepancies are only allowed if required by the Decoder Input and Decoder Output specifications above. \n\nCarefully verify your work after every step. When you find mistakes, document and fix them immediately. Be critical of results **after every step of processing**. \n\nYou will be assessed on whether the decisions you make on how to load, filter, process, align, reformat, and save the data are reasonable and match the reference paper and code and these instructions. During testing, the reference version of `train_decoder.py` will be run on your converted data. Data statistics and decoder accuracy will be compared to those achieved by human-written conversion code. \n\n---\n\n## Conversion Workflow\n\n**IMPORTANT**: Follow these steps in order. Save plots as `processing_<session_id>.png`.\n- Reuse code from reference code bases where appropriate\n- Write efficient code:\n  - Vectorize loops\n  - Avoid unnecessary file I/O\n  - Use parallel processing if beneficial\n  - Print timing information to find bottlenecks\n- Plots for `--show-processing` mode should visually convince the user that every step of the conversion is correct.\n  - There are no temporal misalignments\n  - Discretization of continuous outputs is correct\n- Write clear, well-documented functions\n- Handle missing data appropriately (consult references, use sensible defaults, document)\n- Validate data shapes and types at each step\n- Include sanity checks (e.g., trial counts match across arrays)\n\n**Done when**: `convert_data.py` exists and runs efficiently without errors.\n\n**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 7 until `convert_data.py` exists and you have updated Step 6 in CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n\n---\n\n### Step 7: Sample Conversion and Validation\n\n**Goal**: Validate the conversion on a small sample before processing the full dataset.\n\n**Actions**:\n1. Run `python -u convert_data.py sample_data.pkl --sample --show-processing 2>&1 | tee conversion_sample_out.txt`\n  a. Verify the sample data structure matches the specification.\n  b. If it is much longer than your previous estimate (> 1.5x), kill the process, optimize bottlenecks, and repeat. \n5. Run `python -u train_decoder.py converted_data.pkl --verify-only 2>&1 | tee verification_full_out.txt`. \n6. Check that no data was lost during conversion \n7. Spot-check a few sessions to verify data integrity\n8. **Check for consistency** between dataset statistics in `verification_full_out.txt` and the reference texts\n9. Investigate any inconsistencies, and revise the conversion script until all dataset statistics are consistent\n\n**Done when**:\n- `converted_data.pkl` is created and passes manual inspection\n- `verification_full_out.txt` is created and passes inspection\n- **ALL** dataset statistics are consistent with values from reference texts\n- You have documented statistics and consistency in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 9\"\n\n**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 10 until `converted_data.pkl` and `verification_full_out.txt` files exist and CONVERSION_NOTES.MD Step 9 is filled in.\n\n---\n\n### Step 10: Critical Review 1\n\n**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain.\n\n**Check 1: Output log verification**\nRead through `verification_full_out.txt` to find and fix **all errors**. Address each warning by either fixing its cause or verifying it that it is not possible to fix it. Explain why each warning could not be fixed in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n\n**Check 2: Construct sanity checks**\n- Develop at least one sanity check each on neural, input, and output data.\n- These must involve loading in the original data files (NOT through your conversion code).\n- Construct comparison tests and criteria using `np.allclose()`. \n- These tests can be spot-checks of the data, e.g. neural activity at trial 5, timepoint 10, neuron 3. \n- Fix all mismatches/sanity check failures. \n- Document your sanity checks to `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n\n**Check 3: Reference code comparison**\n- Compare your code to the reference code and methods. \n- For each major processing step — (a) data loading, (b) neuron/trial filtering, (c) temporal alignment, (d) binning, (e) input construction, (f) output construction — identify the corresponding code in your script and in the reference, and compare the logic. \n- Check whether you use the same variables, filtering, and processing as the reference code. \n- Fix any mismatches. \n- Document your comparisons in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n- Explain your reasoning for any differences in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`.\n\n**Check 4: Key statistics comparison**\n- Verify that key statistics (number of animals, sessions, trials, neurons, input and output ranges, output distributions) match information available in the reference papers. \n- Write code to investigate any discrepancies. \n- Fix any discovered issues. \n- Document your comparisons in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n\n**Check 5: Check for edge cases**\n- Check for off-by-one errors at the beginning/ends of trials, sessions, and subjects, or in definitions of the inputs and outputs. \n- There may be minor issues in the data that your code must handle. Check that your code robustly handles issues in the original data. \n\n**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\n1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\n2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\n3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\n4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\n\nWrite a report to CONVERSION_NOTES.md describing **every** check you did, to help convince the user that the conversion code works. **Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 10\".\n\n**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 11 until you have checked for consistency between **every** statistic in the reference texts, code, and data and documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Step 10. \n\n---\n\n### Step 11: Full Decoder Training\n\n**Goal**: Validate the decoder on the **complete** dataset.\n\n**⚠️ IMPORTANT**: This step must complete fully. Do not skip or abbreviate. Do not proceed to Step 12 until this step is completely finished.\n\n**Actions**:\n1. Run `python -u train_decoder.py converted_data.pkl --plot-samples 2>&1 | tee train_decoder_full_out.txt`. If the GPU does not have sufficient RAM for the network training, use the flag `--cpu` to specify to use the CPU to train. \n2. **Wait for complete execution** — this may take significant time for large datasets\n3. **Check training**: Verify loss decreases over epochs\n4. **Check accuracy**: High accuracy is an indicator that data conversion has been done correctly. Accuracy near chance is a sign that there might be issues in temporal alignment of signals, choice of data streams, or filtering or processing of data. Compare accuracy to expectations based on the papers. \n\n**Done when**: \n1. Full decoder training completes (the script finishes running)\n2. Accuracy results are documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 11\" with a table of accuracies\n\n---\n\n### Step 12: Critical Review 2\n\n**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain. Perform all of the following checks:\n\n**Check 1: Accuracy vs chance analysis**\n- For each output, compare validation accuracy to chance (1/num_classes)\n- Accuracy below chance indicates a bug, investigate and fix conversion issues to improve this. \n- Accuracy below 1.5x chance may also indicate a bug, investigate whether changes in conversion logic could improve accuracy. \n\n**Check 2: Accuracy comparison to papers**\n- Find every decoding accuracy reported in the reference papers\n- Create a table comparing your accuracy to the papers's accuracy for each variable\n- If your accuracy is lower than the reported accuracy, this may indicate a bug in your conversion that you must fix. \n- Differences in algorithm/architecture should be relatively small because of the data dimensionality and size. Do not use this as an excuse to dismiss differences, investigate them completely before moving on. \n\n**Check 3: Train vs validation gap**\n- If training accuracy is much higher (>1.5x) than validation accuracy for any output, investigate overfitting or data leakage. \n\n**If accuracy is low for an output, try these specific debugging steps:**\n1. Verify the output values are correct by loading raw data and checking 3 specific trials\n2. Check temporal alignment — plot neural + output for a single trial to verify they're synchronized\n3. Check whether the output has enough variation (not 99% one class)\n4. Check that filtering steps for neural activity streams are followed. \n5. Check that processing of the data matches the reference code and papers. \n\n**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\n1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\n2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\n3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\n4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\n\n**Report all checks and results** to `CONVERSION_NOTES.md` to convince the user that the conversion and decoding has been done correctly. \n\n**Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 12\".\n\n---\n\n### Step 13: Documentation and Cleanup\n\n**Goal**: Finalize documentation and organize files.\n\n**Actions**:\n- Ensure CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete with all:\n  - Key decisions and rationale\n  - Checks for correctness of the conversion\n  - Validation results and tables\n  - Any issues found and how they were resolved\n- Create a user-facing `README.md` summarizing:\n  - Dataset description\n  - How to load and use the converted data\n  - Output format specification\n  - Key statistics\n- Move analysis/investigation scripts to a `cache/` folder\n- Create `cache/README_CACHE.md` documenting cached files\n\n**Done when**: README.md exists, CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete, and directory is clean.\n\n---\n\n## CONVERSION_NOTES.md Template\n\n**Create this file IMMEDIATELY in Step 0.   \"\"\"\n   246→    Select spiking data for specified interval in each recording.\n   247→\n   248→    Parameters\n   249→    ----------\n   250→    times : array-like\n   251→        time in seconds  Returns\n   263→    -------\n   264→    tuple\n   265→        - (list): time in seconds for each interval; timepoints refer to the start/left edge of a bin\n   266→        - (list): data for each multiprocessing to work with nested functions\n   278→    @globalize\n   279→    def compute_spike_count(interval):\n   280→        # We use interval_idx to track the interval order while working with p.imap_unordered()\n   281→        interval_idx, t_beg, t_end = interval\n   282→        idxs_t = (times >= t_beg) & (times < t_end)\n   283→        times_curr = times[idxs_t]\n   284→        clust_curr = clusters[idxs_t]\n   285→        if times_curr.shape[0] == 0:\n   286→            # no spikes in this trial\n   287→            binned_spikes_tmp = np.zeros((n_clusters_in_region, Specifically, if one instance offline,\n        you need to pass 'eid' and 'pname' here as they cannot be inferred from pid in offline mode.\n\n    Returns\n    -------\n    selected_spikes: dict\n        Spike trains associated with clusters. Dictionary with keys ['depths', 'times', 'clusters', 'amps']\n    selected_clusters: pandas.DataFrame\n        Information of clusters for this pid \n    sampling_freq: float\n        Sampling frequency of spiking data (action potential)\n    \"\"\"\n    eid = kwargs.pop('eid', '')\n    pname = kwargs.pop('pname', '')\n    spike_loader = SpikeSortingLoader(pid=pid, one=one, eid=eid, pname=pname)\n    sampling_freq = spike_loader.raw_electrophysiology(band=\"ap\", stream=True).fs\n    \n    spikes, clusters, channels = spike_loader.load_spike_sorting()\n    clusters_labeled = SpikeSortingLoader.merge_clusters(\n        spikes, clusters, channels, compute_metrics=compute_metrics).to_df()\n    if qc is None:\n        return spikes, clusters_labeled, sampling_freq\n    else:\n        iok = clusters_labeled['label'] >= qc\n        selected_clusters = clusters_labeled[iok]\n        spike_idx, ib = ismember(spikes['clusters'], selected_clusters.index)\n        selected_clusters.reset_index(drop=True, inplace=True)\n        selected_spikes = {k: v[spike_idx] for k, v in spikes.items()}\n        selected_spikes['clusters'] = selected_clusters.index[ib].astype(np.int32)\n        return selected_spikes, selected_clusters, sampling_freq\n\n\ndef merge_probes(spikes_list, clusters_list):\n    \"\"\"\n    Merge spikes and clusters information from several probes as if they were recorded from the same probe.\n    This can be used to account for the fact that data from the probes recorded in the same session are not\n    statistically independent as they have the same underlying behaviour.\n\n    NOTE: The clusters dataframe will be re-indexed to avoid duplicated indices. Accordingly, spikes['clusters']\n    will be updated. To unambiguously identify clusters use the column 'uuids'\n\n    Parameters\n    ----------\n    spikes_list: list of dicts\n        List of spike dictionaries as loaded by SpikeSortingLoader or brainwidemap.load_good_units\n    clusters_list: list of pandas.DataFrames\n        List of cluster dataframes as loaded by SpikeSortingLoader.merge_clusters or brainwidemap.load_good_units\n\n    Returns\n    -------\n    merged_spikes: dict\n        Merged and time-sorted spikes in single dictionary, where 'clusters' is adjusted to index into merged_clusters\n    merged_clusters: pandas.DataFrame\n        Merged clusters in single dataframe, re-indexed to avoid duplicate indices.\n        To unambiguously identify clusters use the column 'uuids'\n    \"\"\"\n\n    assert (len(clusters_list) == len(spikes_list)), 'clusters_list and spikes_list must have the same length'\n    assert all([isinstance(s, dict) for s in spikes_list]), 'spikes_list must contain only dictionaries'\n    assert all([isinstance(c, pd.DataFrame) for c in clusters_list]), 'clusters_list must contain only pd.DataFrames'\n\n    merged_spikes = []\n    merged_clusters = []\n    cluster_max = 0\n\n    for clusters, spikes in zip(clusters_list, spikes_list):\n        spikes['clusters'] += cluster_max\n        cluster_max = clusters.index.max() + 1\n        merged_spikes.append(spikes)\n        merged_clusters.append(clusters)\n        \n    merged_clusters = pd.concat(merged_clusters, ignore_index=True)\n    merged_spikes = {k: np.concatenate([s[k] for s in merged_spikes]) for k in merged_spikes[0].keys()}\n    # Sort spikes by spike time\n    sort_idx = np.argsort(merged_spikes['times'], kind='stable')\n    merged_spikes = {k: v[sort_idx] for k, v in merged_spikes.items()}\n\n    return merged_spikes, merged_clusters\n\n\ndef load_trials_and_mask(\n        one, eid, min_rt=0.08, max_rt=2., nan_exclude='default', min_trial_len=None,\n        max_trial_len=None, exclude_unbiased=False, exclude_nochoice=True, sess_loader=None):\n    \"\"\"\n    Function to load all trials for a given session and create a mask to exclude all trials that have a reaction time\n    shorter than min_rt or longer than max_rt or that have NaN for one of the specified events.\n\n    Parameters\n    ----------\n    one: one.api.ONE\n        Instance to be used to connect to local or remote database\n    eid: str\n        A session UUID\n    min_rt: float or None\n        Minimum admissible reaction time in seconds for a trial to be included. Default is 0.08. If None, don't apply.\n    max_rt: float or None\n        Maximum admissible reaction time in seconds for a trial to be included. Default is 2. If None, don't apply.\n    nan_exclude: list or 'default'\n        List of trial events that cannot be NaN for a trial to be included. If set to 'default' the list is\n        ['stimOn_times','choice','feedback_times','probabilityLeft','firstMovement_times','feedbackType']\n    min_trial_len: float or None\n        Minimum admissible trial length measured by goCue_time (start) and feedback_time (end).\n        Default is None.\n    max_trial_len: float or Nona\n        Maximum admissible trial length measured by goCue_time (start) and feedback_time (end).\n        Default is None.\n    exclude_unbiased: bool\n        True to exclude trials that fall within the unbiased block at the beginning of session.\n        Default is False.\n    exclude_nochoice: bool\n        True to exclude trials where the animal does not respond. Default is False.\n    sess_loader: brainbox.io.one.SessionLoader or NoneType\n        Optional SessionLoader object; if None, this object will be created internally\n\n    Returns\n    -------\n    trials: pandas.DataFrame\n        Trials table containing all trials for this session. If complete with columns:\n        ['stimOff_times','goCueTrigger_times','feedbackType','contrastLeft','contrastRight','rewardVolume',\n        'goCue_times','choice','feedback_times','stimOn_times','response_times','firstMovement_times',\n        'probabilityLeft', 'intervals_0', 'intervals_1']\n    mask: pandas.Series\n        Boolean Series to mask trials table for trials that pass specified criteria. True for all trials that should be\n        included, False for all trials that should be excluded.\n    \"\"\"\n\n    if nan_exclude == 'default':\n        nan_exclude = [\n            'stimOn_times',\n            'choice',\n            'feedback_times',\n            'probabilityLeft',\n            'firstMovement_times',\n            'feedbackType'\n        ]\n\n    if sess_loader is None:\n        sess_loader = SessionLoader(one, eid)\n\n    if sess_loader.trials.empty:\n        sess_loader.load_trials()\n\n    # Create a mask for trials to exclude\n    # Remove trials that are outside the allowed reaction time range\n    if min_rt is not None:\n        query = f'(firstMovement_times - stimOn_times < {min_rt})'\n    else:\n        query = ''\n    if max_rt is not None:\n        query += f' | (firstMovement_times - stimOn_times > {max_rt})'\n    # Remove trials that are outside the allowed trial duration range\n    if min_trial_len is not None:\n        query += f' | 0.5)'\n    # Remove trials where animal does not respond\n    if exclude_nochoice:\n        query += ' | (choice == 0)'\n    # If min_rt was None we have to clean up the string\n    if min_rt is None:\n        query = query[3:]\n\n    # Create mask\n    mask = ~sess_loader.trials.eval(query)\n\n    return sess_loader.trials, mask\n\n\ndef list_brain_regions(neural_dict, **kwargs):\n create_intervals(start_time, end_time, interval_len):\n    interval_begs = np.arange(\n        start_time, end_time-interval_len, interval_len\n    )\n    interval_ends = np.arange(\n        start_time+interval_len, end_time, interval_len\n    )\n    return np.c_[interval_begs, interval_ends]\n\n\ndef get_spike_data_per_interval(times, clusters, interval_begs, interval_ends, interval_len, binsize, n_workers=os.cpu_count()):\n    \"\"\"\n    Select spiking data for specified interval in each recording.\n\n    Parameters\n    ----------\n    times : array-like\n        time in seconds for each spike\n    clusters : array-like\n        cluster id for each spike\n    interval_begs : array-like\n        beginning of each interval in seconds\n    interval_ends : array-like\n        end of each interval in seconds\n    interval_len : float\n    binsize : float\n        width of each bin in seconds\n\n    Returns\n    -------\n    tuple\n        - (list): time in seconds for each interval; timepoints refer to the start/left edge of a bin\n        - (list): data for each interval of shape (n_clusters, n_bins)\n\n    \"\"\"\n    n_intervals = len(interval_begs)\n\n    # np.ceil because we want to make sure our bins contain all data\n    n_bins = int(np.ceil(interval_len / binsize))\n\n    cluster_ids = np.unique(clusters)\n    n_clusters_in_region = len(cluster_ids)\n\n    # This allows multiprocessing to work with nested functions\n    @globalize\n    def compute_spike_count(interval):\n        # We use interval_idx to track the interval order while working with p.imap_unordered()\n        interval_idx, t_beg, t_end = interval\n        idxs_t = (times >= t_beg) & (times < t_end)\n        times_curr = times[idxs_t]\n        clust_curr = clusters[idxs_t]\n        if times_curr.shape[0] == 0:\n            # no spikes in this trial\n            binned_spikes_tmp = np.zeros((n_clusters_in_region, n_bins))\n            if np.isnan(t_beg) or np.isnan(t_end):\n                t_idxs = np.nan * np.ones(n_bins)\n            else:\n                t_idxs = np.arange(t_beg, t_end + binsize / 2, binsize)\n            idxs_tmp = np.arange(n_clusters_in_region)\n        else:\n            # bin spikes\n            binned_spikes_tmp, t_idxs, cluster_idxs = bincount2D(\n                times_curr, clust_curr, xbin=binsize, xlim=[t_beg, t_end])\n            # find indices of clusters that returned spikes for this trial\n            _, idxs_tmp, _ = np.intersect1d(cluster_ids, cluster_idxs, return_indices=True)\n        return binned_spikes_tmp[:, :n_bins], idxs_tmp, interval_idx\n\n    binned_spikes = np.zeros((n_intervals, n_clusters_in_region, n_bins))\n    with multiprocessing.Pool(processes=n_workers) as p:\n        intervals = list(zip(np.arange(n_intervals), interval_begs, interval_ends))\n        with tqdm(total=len(intervals)) as pbar:\n            for res in p.imap_unordered(compute_spike_count, intervals):\n                pbar.update()\n                binned_spikes[res[-1], res[1], :] += res[0]\n        pbar.close()\n        p.close()\n    return binned_spikes\n\n\ndef bin_spiking_data(reg_clu_ids, neural_df,    target_times, \n    target_vals, \n    intervals=None, \n    trials_df=None, \n    allow_nans=False, \n    n_workers=os.cpu_count(), \n    **kwargs\n):\n    \"\"\"\n    Format a single session-wide array of target data into a list of interval-based arrays.\n\n    Note: the bin size of the returned data will only be equal to the input `binsize` if that value\n    evenly divides `align_interval`; for example if `align_interval=(0, 0.2)` and `binsize=0.10`,\n    then the returned data will have the correct binsize. If `align_interval=(0, 0.2)` and\n    `binsize=0.06` then the returned data will not have the correct binsize.\n\n    Parameters\n    ----------\n    target_times : array-like\n        time in seconds for each sample\n    target_vals : array-like\n        data samples\n    intervals : \n        array of time intervals for each recording chunk including trials and non-trials\n    trials_df : pd.DataFrame\n        requires a column that matches `align_event`\n    align_event : str\n        event to align interval to\n        firstMovement_times | stimOn_times | feedback_times\n    align_interval : tuple\n        (align_begin, align_end); time in seconds relative to align_event\n    binsize : float\n        size of individual bins in interval\n    allow_nans : bool, optional\n        False to skip intervals with >0 NaN values in target data\n\n    Returns\n    -------\n    tuple\n        - (list): time in seconds for each interval\n        - (list): data for each interval\n        - (array-like): mask of good intervals (True) and bad intervals (False)\n\n    \"\"\"\n\n    binsize = kwargs['binsize']\n    align_interval = kwargs['time_window']\n    interval_len = align_interval[1] - align_interval[0]\n\n    if trials_df is not None:\n        align_event = kwargs['align_time']\n        align_times = trials_df[align_event].values\n        interval_begs = align_times + align_interval[0]\n        interval_ends = align_times + align_interval[1]\n    else:\n        assert intervals is not None, 'Require intervals to segment the recording into chunks including trials and non-trials.'\n        interval_begs, interval_ends = intervals.T\n\n    n_intervals = len(interval_begs)\n\n    if np.all(np.isnan(interval_begs)) or np.all(np.isnan(interval_ends)):\n        print('interval times all nan')\n        good_interval = np.nan * np.ones(interval_begs.shape[0])\n        target_times_list = []\n        target_vals_list = []\n        return target_times_list, target_vals_list, good_interval\n\n    # np.ceil because we want to make sure our bins contain all data\n    n_bins = int(np.ceil(interval_len / binsize))\n\n    # split data into intervals\n    idxs_beg = np.searchsorted(target_times, interval_begs, side='right')\n    idxs_end = np.searchsorted(target_times, interval_ends, side='left')\n    target_times_og_list = [target_times[ib:ie] for ib, ie in zip(idxs_beg, idxs_end)]\n    target_vals_og_list = [target_vals[ib:ie] for ib, ie in zip(idxs_beg, idxs_end)]\n\n    # interpolate and the interval order while working with p.imap_unordered()\n        interval_idx, target_time, target_vals = target\n\n        is_good_interval, x_interp, y_interp = False, None, None\n        \n        if len(target_vals) == 0:\n            skip_reason = 'target data not present'\n            return interval_idx, is_good_interval, x_interp, y_interp, skip_reason\n        if np.sum(np.isnan(target_vals)) > 0 and not allow_nans:\n            skip_reason = 'nans in target data'\n            return interval_idx, is_good_interval, x_interp, y_interp, skip_reason\n        if np.isnan(interval_begs[interval_idx]) or np.isnan(interval_ends[interval_idx]):\n            skip_reason = 'bad interval data'\n            return interval_idx, is_good_interval, x_interp, y_interp, skip_reason\n        if np.abs(interval_begs[interval_idx] - target_time[0]) > binsize:\n            skip_reason = 'target data starts too late'\n            return interval_idx, is_good_interval, x_interp, y_interp, skip_reason\n        if np.abs(interval_ends[interval_idx] - target_time[-1]) > binsize:\n            skip_reason = 'target data ends too early'\n            return interval_idx, is_good_interval, x_interp, y_interp, skip_reason\n\n        is_good_interval, skip_reason = True, None\n        x_interp = np.linspace(interval_begs[interval_idx] + binsize, binned_spikes\n   311→\n   312→\n   313→def bin_spiking_data(reg_clu_ids, neural_df, intervals=None, trials_df=None, n_workers=os.cpu_count(), **kwargs):\n   314→    \"\"\"\n   315→    Format a single list\n   341→        each element is a 2D numpy.ndarray for a single interval of shape (n_bins, n_clusters)\n   342→    array\n   343→        cluster ids that account for axis 1 of the above 2D trials_df[kwargs['align_time']] + kwargs['time_window'][0],\n   350→            trials_df[kwargs['align_time']] + kwargs['time_window'][1]\n   351→        ]).T\n   352→        chunk_len = 357→        assert intervals is not None, 'Require intervals to segment the recording into chunks including trials and non-trials.'\n   358→        chunk_len = intervals[0,1] - intervals[0,0]\n   359→ clusters_used_in_bins = np.unique(regclu)\n   368→\n   369→    binsize = kwargs.get('binsize', chunk_len)\n   370→    \n   371→    if chunk_len / binsize == 1.0:\n   372→        # one vector of neural return beh_dict\n   505→\n   506→\n   507→def get_behavior_per_interval(\n   508→    target_times, \n   509→    target_vals, \n   510→    intervals=None, \n   511→    trials_df=None, \n   512→    allow_nans=False, \n   513→    n_workers=os.cpu_count(), \n   514→    **kwargs\n   515→):\n   516→    \"\"\"\n   517→    Format a single session-wide array of target data into a list of interval-based arrays.\n   518→\n   519→    Note: the bin size of the returned data will only be equal to the input `binsize` if that value\n   520→    evenly divides `align_interval`; for example if `align_interval=(0, 0.2)` and `binsize=0.10`,\n   521→    then the returned data will have the correct binsize. If `align_interval=(0, 0.2)` and\n   522→    `binsize=0.06` then the returned data will not have the correct binsize.\n   523→\n   524→    Parameters\n   525→    ----------\n   526→    target_times : array-like\n   527→        time in seconds for each sample\n   528→    target_vals : array-like\n   529→        data samples\n   530→    intervals : \n   531→        array of time intervals for each recording chunk including trials and non-trials\n   532→    trials_df : pd.DataFrame\n   533→        requires a column that matches `align_event`\n   534→    align_event : str\n   535→        event to align interval to\n   536→        firstMovement_times | stimOn_times | feedback_times\n   537→    align_interval : tuple\n   538→        (align_begin, align_end); time in seconds relative to align_event\n   539→    binsize : float\n   540→        size of individual bins in interval\n   541→    allow_nans : bool, optional\n   542→        False to skip intervals with >0 NaN values in target data\n   543→\n   544→    Returns\n   545→    -------\n   546→    tuple\n   547→        - (list): time in seconds for each interval\n   548→        - (list): data for each interval\n   549→        - (array-like): mask of good intervals (True) and bad intervals (False)\n   550→\n   551→    \"\"\"\n   552→\n   553→    binsize = kwargs['binsize']\n   554→    align_interval = kwargs['time_window']\n   555→    interval_len = align_interval[1] - align_interval[0]\n   556→\n   557→    if trials_df is not None:\n   558→        align_event = kwargs['align_time']\n   559→        align_times = trials_df[align_event].values\n   560→        interval_begs = align_times + align_interval[0]\n   561→        interval_ends = align_times + align_interval[1]\n   562→    else:\n   563→        assert intervals is not None, 'Require intervals to segment the recording into chunks including trials and non-trials.'\n   564→        interval_begs, interval_ends = intervals.T\n   565→\n   566→    n_intervals = len(interval_begs)\n   567→\n   568→    if np.all(np.isnan(interval_begs)) or np.all(np.isnan(interval_ends)):\n   569→        print('interval times all nan')\n   570→        good_interval = np.nan * np.ones(interval_begs.shape[0])\n   571→        target_times_list = []\n   572→        target_vals_list = []\n   573→        return target_times_list, target_vals_list, good_interval\n   574→\n   575→    # np.ceil because we want to make sure our bins contain all data\n   576→    n_bins = int(np.ceil(interval_len / binsize))\n   577→\n   578→    # split data into intervals\n   579→    idxs_beg = np.searchsorted(target_times, interval_begs, side='right')\n   580→    idxs_end = np.searchsorted(target_times, interval_ends, side='left')\n   581→    target_times_og_list = [target_times[ib:ie] for ib, ie in zip(idxs_beg, idxs_end)]\n   582→    target_vals_og_list = [target_vals[ib:ie] for ib, ie in zip(idxs_beg, idxs_end)]\n   583→\n   584→    # interpolate and store\n   585→    target_times_list = [None for _ in range(len(target_times_og_list))]\n   586→    target_vals_list = [None for _ in range(len(target_times_og_list))]\n   587→    good_interval = [None for _ in range(len(target_times_og_list))]\n   588→    skip_reasons = [None for _ in range(len(target_times_og_list))]\n   589→\n   590→    @globalize\n   591→    def interpolate_behavior(target):\n   592→        # We use interval_idx to track the interval order while working with p.imap_unordered()\n   593→        interval_idx, target_time, target_vals = target\n   594→\n   595→        is_good_interval, x_interp, y_interp = False, None, None\n   596→        \n   597→        if len(target_vals) == 0:\n   598→            skip_reason = 'target data not present'\n   599→            return interval_idx, is_good_interval, x_interp, y_interp, skip_reason\n   600→        if np.sum(np.isnan(target_vals)) > 0 and not allow_nans:\n   601→            skip_reason = 'nans in target data'\n   602→            return interval_idx, is_good_interval, x_interp, y_interp, skip_reason\n   603→        if np.isnan(interval_begs[interval_idx]) or np.isnan(interval_ends[interval_idx]):\n   604→            skip_reason = 'bad interval data'\n   605→            return interval_idx, is_good_interval, x_interp, y_interp, skip_reason\n   606→        if np.abs(interval_begs[interval_idx] - target_time[0]) > binsize:\n   607→            skip_reason = 'target data starts too late'\n   608→            return interval_idx, is_good_interval, x_interp, y_interp, skip_reason\n   609→        if np.abs(interval_ends[interval_idx] - target_time[-1]) > binsize:\n   610→            skip_reason = 'target data ends too early'\n   611→            return interval_idx, is_good_interval, x_interp, y_interp, skip_reason\n   612→\n   613→        is_good_interval, skip_reason = True, None\n   614→        x_interp = np.linspace(interval_begs[interval_idx] + binsize, interval_ends[interval_idx], n_bins)\n   615→        if len(target_vals.shape) > 1 and target_vals.shape[1] > 1:\n   616→            n_dims = target_vals.shape[1]\n   617→            y_interp_tmps = []\n   618→            for n in range(n_dims):\n   619→                y_interp_tmps.append(interp1d(\n   620→                    target_time, target_vals[:, n], kind='linear',\n   621→                    fill_value='extrapolate')(x_interp))\n   622→            y_interp = np.hstack([y[:, None] for y in y_interp_tmps])\n   623→        else:\n   624→            y_interp = interp1d(\n   625→                target_time, target_vals, kind='linear', fill_value='extrapolate')(x_interp)\n   626→        return interval_idx, is_good_interval, x_interp, y_interp, skip_reason\n   627→\n   628→    with multiprocessing.Pool(processes=n_workers) as p:\n   629→        targets = list(zip(np.arange(n_intervals), target_times_og_list, target_vals_og_list))\n   630→        with tqdm(total=n_intervals) as pbar:\n   631→            for res in p.imap_unordered(interpolate_behavior, targets):\n   632→                pbar.update()\n   633→                good_interval[res[0]] = res[1]\n   634→                target_times_list[res[0]] = res[2]\n   635→                target_vals_list[res[0]] = res[3]\n   636→                skip_reasons[res[0]] = res[-1]\n   637→        pbar.close()\n   638→        p.close()\n   639→\n   640→    return target_times_list, target_vals_list, np.array(good_interval), skip_reasons    \n   641→\n   642→\n   643→def load_anytime_behaviors(one, eid, n_workers=os.cpu_count()):\n   644→\n   645→    behaviors = [\n   646→        'wheel-velocity', 'wheel-speed',\n   647→        'left-whisker-motion-energy', 'right-whisker-motion-energy',\n   648→        'left-pupil-diameter', 'right-pupil-diameter',\n   649→    ]\n   650→\n   651→    @globalize\n   652→    def load_beh(beh):\n   653→        return beh, load_target_behavior(one, eid, beh)\n   654→    \n   655→    behave_dict = {}\n   656→    with multiprocessing.Pool(processes=n_workers) as p:\n   657→        with tqdm(total=len(behaviors)) as pbar:\n   658→            for res in p.imap_unordered(load_beh, behaviors):\n   659→                pbar.update()\n   660→                behave_dict.update({res[0]: res[1]})\n   661→        pbar.close()\n   662→        p.close()\n   663→    \n   664→    return behave_dict\n   665→\n   666→\n   667→def bin_behaviors(\n   668→    one, \n   669→    eid, \n   670→    behaviors,\n   671→    intervals=None, \n   672→    trials_only=False, \n   673→    trials_df=None, \n   674→    mask=None, \n   675→    allow_nans=True, \n   676→    n_workers=os.cpu_count(),\n   677→    **kwargs\n   678→):\n   679→\n   680→    behave_dict, mask_dict = {}, {}\n   681→    \n   682→    if mask is not None:\n   683→        trials_df = trials_df[mask]\n   684→\n   685→    if trials_df is not None:        \n   686→        choice = trials_df['choice'].to_numpy()\n   687→        block = trials_df['probabilityLeft'].to_numpy()\n   688→        reward = (trials_df['rewardVolume'] > 1).astype(int).to_numpy()\n   689→        contrast = np.c_[trials_df['contrastLeft'], trials_df['contrastRight']]\n   690→        contrast = (-1 * np.nan_to_num(contrast, 0)).sum(1)\n   691→\n   692→        behave_dict.update(\n   693→            {'choice': choice, 'block': block, 'reward': reward, 'contrast': contrast}\n   694→        )\n   695→        behave_mask = np.ones(len(trials_df)) \n   696→    else:\n   697→        assert intervals is not None, 'Require intervals to segment the recording into chunks including trials and non-trials.'\n   698→        behave_mask = np.ones(len(intervals)) \n   699→        \n   700→    for beh in behaviors:\n   701→        if beh == 'whisker-motion-energy':\n   702→            target_dict = load_target_behavior(one, eid, 'left-whisker-motion-energy')\n   703→            if 'skip' in target_dict.keys():\n   704→                target_dict = load_target_behavior(one, eid, 'right-whisker-motion-energy')  \n   705→        elif beh == 'pupil-diameter':\n   706→            target_dict = load_target_behavior(one, eid, 'left-pupil-diameter')\n   707→            if 'skip' in target_dict.keys():\n   708→ target_vals, intervals=intervals, \n   714→            trials_df=trials_df, allow_nans=allow_nans, n_workers=n_workers, **kwargs\n   715→        )\n   716→        behave_dict.update({beh: np.array(target_vals_list, dtype=object)})\n   717→        mask_dict.update({beh: target_mask})\n   718→        behave_mask = np.logical_and(behave_mask, target_mask)\n   719→\n   720→    if not allow_nans:\n   721→        for k, v in behave_dict.items():\n   722→            behave_dict[k] = behave_dict[beh][behave_mask]\n   723→    \n   724→    return behave_dict, mask_dict\n   725→\n   726→\n   727→def prepare_data(one, eid, bwm_df, params, n_workers=os.cpu_count()):\n   728→    \n   729→    # when merging probes we are interested in eids, not pids\n   730→    pids, probe_names = one.eid2pid(eid)\n   731→    print(f\"Merge {len(probe_names)} probes for session eid: {eid}\")\n   732→\n   733→    clusters_list = []\n   734→    spikes_list = []\n   735→    for pid, probe_name in spikes_list.append(tmp_spikes)\n   739→        clusters_list.append(tmp_clusters)\n   740→    spikes, clusters = merge_probes(spikes_list, clusters_list)\n   741→\n   742→    trials_df, trials_mask = load_trials_and_mask(one=one, eid=eid, max_trial_len=10.0)\n   743→        \n   744→    behave_dict = load_anytime_behaviors(one, eid, n_workers=n_workers)\n   745→    \n   746→    neural_dict = {\n   747→        'spike_times': spikes['times'],\n   748→        'spike_clusters': spikes['clusters'],\n   749→        'cluster_regions': clusters['acronym'].to_numpy(),\n   750→    }\n   751→        \n   752→    meta_data = {\n   753→        'eid': eid,\n   754→        'probe_name': probe_name,\n   755→        'sampling_freq': sampling_freq,\n   756→        'cluster_channels': list(clusters['channels']),\n   757→        'cluster_regions': list(clusters['acronym']),\n   758→        'good_clusters': list((clusters['label'] >= 1).astype(int)),\n   759→        'cluster_depths': list(clusters['depths']),\n   760→        'uuids':  list(clusters['uuids']),\n   761→        'cluster_qc': {k: np.asarray(v) for k, v in clusters.to_dict('list').items()},\n   762→    }\n   763→\n   764→    trials_data = {\n   765→        'trials_df': trials_df,\n   766→        'trials_mask': trials_mask\n   767→    }\n   768→\n   769→    return neural_dict, behave_dict, meta_data, trials_data\n   770→\n   771→\n   772→def align_spike_behavior(binned_spikes, binned_behaviors, beh_names, trials_mask=None):\n   773→    \"\"\"Function to verify trial alignment between neural and behavior data.\n {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_017Sb6oGV9cxvuVTjEggX6c8","type":"tool_result","content":"     1→From \"Exploiting correlations across trials and behavioral sessions to improve neural decoding\"\n     2→\n     3→Our goal is to decode behavior y \\in R^P from spike-sorted, temporally binned spike counts. For each trial k, neural activity has size N x T, where N is the number of neurons and T the number of time steps. Recordings are split into 2-s trials, each divided into 20-ms bins, producing T = 100 time steps. For each trial, we constructed X \\in R^{N x T}  by aggregating spike counts and used it to compute a decoder estimate of the true behavior y \\in R^P. y is constant within a trial (“per-trial decoding”); when P = T, y varies across time (“per-timestep decoding”).\n     4→\n     5→We apply our models to 433 IBL sessions,17 covering 270 brain regions and four behavioral variables: choice, prior, wheel speed, and whisker motion energy.\n     6→\n     7→ In the IBL experiments, mice rotate a wheel to indicate the location of a visual stimulus, defined as their choice (Figure 1A). For the first 90 trials, the stimulus appears randomly on either side with equal probability; in subsequent trials, it appears predominantly on one side in blocks. Mice adapt their behavior to these changing probabilities, allowing estimation of each mouse’s trial-by-trial prior belief (prior) about stimulus location. Additionally, wheel speed and whisker motion energy near the whisker pad are recorded. Whisker motion energy is computed as the mean absolute difference between adjacent video frames within a bounding box anchored between nose tip and eye. Choice and prior are static within a trial, while wheel speed and whisker motion energy are time-varying signals sampled at 60 Hz. See STAR Methods sections “data processing” and “hyperparameter selection” for details.\n     8→\n     9→Data processing\n    10→\n    11→IBL trial-aligned dataset\n    12→\n    13→For choice, we align trials to the stimulus onset, considering neural activity from 0.5 s before to 1.5 s post-onset. For prior, we also align trials to the stimulus onset, including neural activity from 0.6 s to 0.1 s pre-onset. The prior represents the mice’s estimate of the stimulus side probability. We use the same decoding window as in the previous study,27 focusing on the period with minimal wheel movements. Within each trial, we segment neural activity into 50-ms non-overlapping time bins. For each time bin, we bin spike counts using all neurons, sorted by Kilosort 2.5,77 from each session. For dynamic behaviors (wheel speed and whisker motion energy), we select the first movement onset as the alignment event, and decode the behavior starting at the alignment event and ending at 1 s after the alignment event. The neural activity within each trial is binned into non-overlapping 20 ms bins. For each time bin, we similarly bin spike counts using all neurons from each session.\n    14→\n    15→IBL trial-unaligned dataset\n    16→\n    17→We focus only on wheel speed and whisker motion energy, as variables like choice and prior are only defined during experimental trials, which are periods when stimuli are presented and mice respond for rewards. Unlike the trial-aligned dataset, we do not align neural activity and behavior to a specific event onset. Instead, we segment the continuous Neuropixels recordings into 2-s snippets to construct the trial-unaligned dataset for each session. These snippets cover intervals both during and outside experimental trials, capturing the repetitive, structured behaviors within trials as well as the more spontaneous behaviors outside of trials.\n    18→\n    19→From \"A brain-wide map of neural activity during complex behaviour\"\n    20→\n    21→We trained 139 mice (94 male and 45 female) on the International Brain Laboratory (IBL) decision-making task (Fig. 1a,b). On each trial, a visual stimulus appeared to the left or right on a screen, and the mouse had to move it to the centre by turning a wheel with its front paws within 60 s (Fig. 1c). After an initial 90 unbiased trials, the prior probability for the stimulus to appear on the left or right side was constant over a block of trials at a ratio of 20:80% (right block) or 80:20% (left block). Blocks lasted for between 20 and 100 trials, which were drawn from a truncated geometric distribution (empirical mean of 51 trials). Block changes were not cued. Stimulus contrast was uniformly sampled from 5 possible values (100, 25, 12.5, 6.25 and 0%). The 0% contrast trials, when no stimulus was presented, were assigned to a left or right side following the probability distribution of the block. This allowed mice to perform above chance by incorporating this prior in their choices. Following a wheel turn, mice received positive feedback in the form of a water reward, or negative feedback in the form of a white-noise pulse and a 2-s time out. The next trial began after a delay, followed by a quiescence period during which the mice had to hold the wheel still.\n    22→\n    23→Recordings\n    24→\n    25→In these mice, we inserted 699 Neuropixels probes (see an example of one recording of three trials in Fig. 1i), following a grid that covered the left hemisphere of the forebrain and midbrain, which typically represent stimuli or actions on the contralateral side, and the right hemisphere of the cerebellum and hindbrain, which typically represent the ipsilateral side (Fig. 2a). Recordings were collected by 12 laboratories in Europe and the USA, with most recordings using 2 simultaneous probe insertions. To ensure reproducibility, one brain location was targeted in every mouse in every laboratory, as described elsewhere25. Only sessions with at least 400 trials were retained for further analyses. Data were uploaded to a central server, preprocessed and shared through a standardized interface. To perform spike sorting on the recordings, we used a version of Kilosort with custom additions. This process produced 621,733 units (including multineuron activity), averaging 889 per probe. To separate individual neurons from clusters of multineuron activity, we then applied stringent quality-control metrics (based on those described in ref. 15), which identified 75,708 well-isolated neurons, averaging 108 per probe.\n    26→\n    27→Methods\n    28→\n    29→n brief, at the beginning of each trial, the mouse was required to not move the wheel for a quiescence period of 400–700 ms. After the quiescence period, a visual stimulus (Gabor patch) appeared on either the left or right (±35° azimuth) of the screen, with a contrast randomly sampled from a predefined set (100, 25, 12.5, 6 or 0%). A 100-ms tone (5-kHz sine wave) was played at stimulus onset. Mice had 60 s to move the wheel and make a response. Stimuli were yoked to the rotation of the response wheel, such that a 1-mm movement of the wheel moved the stimulus by 4 visual degrees. A response was registered if the centre of the stimulus crossed the  ±35° azimuth line from its original position. If the mouse correctly moved the stimulus 35° to the centre of the screen, it immediately received a 3-μl reward; if it incorrectly moved the stimulus 35° away from the centre, it received a time out. If the mouse responded incorrectly or failed to reach either threshold within the 60-s window, a white-noise burst was played for 500 ms and the inter-trial interval was between 1 and 1.5 s. In trials for which the visual stimulus contrast was set to 0%, the mouse had to respond as for any other trial by turning the wheel in the correct direction (assigned according to the statistics of the prevailing block) to receive a reward, but the mouse was not able to perceive whether the stimulus was presented on the left or right side of the screen. The mouse also received feedback (noise burst or reward) on 0% contrast trials.\n    30→\n    31→Each session started with 90 trials in which the probability of a visual stimulus appearing on the left or right side was equal. Specifically, the 100%, 25%, 12.5% and 6% contrast trials were each presented 10 times on each side, and the 0% contrast was presented 10 times in total (that is, the ratio of the 100, 25, 12.5, 6 and 0% contrasts were set at 2, 2, 2, 2 and 1, respectively). The side (and thus correct movement) for the 0% contrast trials was chosen randomly between the right and left with equal probability. This initial block of 90 trials is referred to as the unbiased block (50:50).\n    32→\n    33→After the unbiased block, trials were presented in biased blocks: in right-bias blocks, stimuli appeared on the right on 80% of the trials, whereas in left-bias blocks, stimuli appeared on the right on 20% of the trials. The ratio of the contrasts remained as above (2:2:2:2:1). Whether the first biased block in a session was left or right was randomly chosen, and blocks were then alternated. The length of a block was drawn from an exponential distribution with scale parameter of 60 trials, but truncated to lie between 20 and 100 trials.\n    34→\n    35→The automated shaping protocol for training involved two collections of sessions. In the first session, the animals started performing a version of the task without biased blocks and were then progressively introduced to harder stimuli with weaker contrasts as they became progressively more competent. They also experienced a debiasing protocol, which was intended to dissuade them from persisting with just one of the choices. Once they were performing sufficiently well on all non-zero contrasts, they were faced with the biased blocks. When, in turn, performance on those was adequate (including on 0% contrast trials, which are informed by the block), they graduated to recording. Supplementary Figure 8a shows a joint histogram of the number of sessions the mice took in the first and second collections (these were not correlated). Supplementary Figure 8b shows a joint histogram of the number of sessions the mice took in the second collection and the performance during the recording sessions. These were also not correlated.\n    36→\n    37→Inclusion criteria\n    38→\n    39→We applied a set of inclusion criteria to sessions, probes and neurons to ensure data quality. Supplementary Table 1 lists the consequences of these criteria for the number of sessions and probes that passed the criteria.\n    40→Sessions and insertions\n    41→\n    42→Each Neuropixels insertion was repeated in at least two laboratories, with reproducibility of outcomes across laboratories verified with extensive analyses that we have previously reported25.\n    43→\n    44→Sessions were included in the data release if the mice performed at least 250 trials, with a performance of at least 90% correct on 100% contrast trials for both left and right blocks, and, to be able to analyse the feedback variable, if there were at least 3 trials with incorrect choices (after applying the trial exclusions below). Furthermore, sessions were included in the release only if they reached threshold on a collection of hardware tests (definitions are available from GiHub (https://int-brain-lab.github.io/iblenv/_autosummary/ibllib.qc.task_metrics.html)).\n    45→\n    46→Insertions were excluded if the neural data failed the whole recording per visually assessed criteria of the ‘Recording Inclusion metrics and Guidelines for Optimal Reproducibility’ (RIGOR) from ref. 25, by presenting major artefacts (see examples in ref. 28) or if the probe tract could not be recovered during the histology procedure. Furthermore, only insertions for which alignments had been resolved (see appendix 6 of ref. 25 for definitions) were used in this study.\n    47→\n    48→After applying these criteria, a total of 459 sessions, 699 insertions and 621,733 neurons remained, constituting the publicly released dataset.\n    49→Trials\n    50→\n    51→For the analyses presented here, trials were excluded if one of the following trial events could not be detected: choice, probabilityLeft, feebackType, feeback times, stimON times and firstMovement times. Trials were further excluded if the time between stimulus onset and the first movement of the wheel (the first wheel-movement time) were outside the range of 0.08–2.00 s.\n    52→Neurons and brain regions\n    53→\n    54→Neurons generated by the spike-sorting pipeline were excluded from the analyses presented here if they failed one of the three criteria described in ref. 28 (the single unit computed metrics of RIGOR25): amplitude > 50 μV; noise cut-off < 20 μV; and refractory period violation. Neurons that passed these criteria were termed well-isolated neurons (or often just ‘neurons’) in this study. Out of the 621,733 units collected, 75,708 were considered well-isolated neurons. Final analyses were additionally restricted to regions that were designated grey matter in the adult mouse Allen Common Coordinate framework6, contained at least five well-isolated neurons per session and were recorded from in at least two such sessions.\n    55→\n    56→Video analysis\n    57→\n    58→We briefly describe the video analysis pipeline (full details can be found in ref. 105). The recording rigs contained three cameras: one called ‘left’ at full resolution (1,280 × 1,024) and 60 Hz, filming the mouse from one side; one called ‘right’, filming the mouse symmetrically from the other side at half resolution (640 × 512) and 150 Hz; and one called ‘body’ at half resolution and 30 Hz, filming the body of the mouse from above. We developed several quality-control metrics to detect raw video issues such as poor illumination (as infrared light bulbs broke) or accidental misplacement of the cameras105.\n    59→\n    60→We computed the motion energy (the mean across pixels of the absolute value of the difference between adjacent frames) of the whisker pad areas in the ‘left’ and ‘right’ videos (Fig. 1d). The whisker pad area was empirically defined using a rectangular bounding box anchored between the nose tip and the eye, both found using DeepLabCut106 (DLC; see more below). This metric quantifies motion in the whisker pad area and has a temporal resolution of the respective camera.\n    61→\n    62→We also performed markerless pose estimation of body parts using DLC31, which is used in a fully automated pipeline in IBL (v.2.1) to track various body parts such as the paws, nose, tongue and pupil (Fig. 1d). In all analyses using DLC estimates, we excluded predictions with likelihood < 0.9. Furthermore, we developed several quality-control metrics for the DLC traces105.\n    63→\n    64→Overview of decoding\n    65→\n    66→We performed a decoding analysis to measure how much information the activity of populations of neurons contained about task variables such as stimulus side and choice. To do this we, used cross-validated, maximum-likelihood regression with L1 regularization (to zero out the contribution from noisy neurons). The neural regressors were defined by binning the spike counts from each neuron in each session in a given region in a specific time window on each trial. The duration of the time window, the number of bins in that time window (that is, the bin size) and the trial event to which it was aligned depended on the variable that is the target of our regression (Supplementary Table 2). These factors are discussed further below and include a variety of behavioural and task variables: stimulus side, choice, feedback and wheel speed and velocity. Although a session may have included multiple probe insertions, we did not perform decoding on these probes separately because they are not independent. Instead, neurons in the same session and region were combined across probes for our decoding analysis. Decoding was cross-validated and compared with a null distribution to test for significance. A given region may have been recorded on multiple sessions; therefore, in the main figures (Figs. 4–7) the region P value was defined by combining session P values using Fisher’s combined probability test, and the region effect size was defined by subtracting the median of the null distribution from the decoding score and reporting the median of the resulting values across sessions. The P values for all regions were then subjected to FDR correction for multiple comparisons at q = 0.01.\n    67→\n    68→Decoding target variables\n    69→\n    70→Stimulus side, choice and feedback were treated as binary target variables for logistic regression. For stimulus side, trials that had zero contrast were excluded. We used the LogisticRegression module from scikit-learn111 (v.1.1.2) with 0.001 tolerance, 20,000 maximum iterations, “l1” penalty, “liblinear” solver and “fit_intercept” set to True. We balanced decoder classes by weighting samples by the inverse of the class frequency, 1/(2Pi,class). Decoding performance was evaluated using the balanced accuracy of classification, which is the average of the recall probabilities for the two classes. Supplementary Figure 9 shows histograms of the regression coefficients for all the variables.\n    71→\n    72→Wheel values (speed and velocity) change over the course of a trial, unlike the previous decoding targets, and we therefore had to treat these target variables differently. We averaged wheel values in nonoverlapping 20-ms bins, starting 200 ms before first wheel-movement time and ending at 1,000 ms after first wheel-movement time. Spike counts were similarly binned. The target value for a given bin (ending at time t) was decoded from spikes in a preceding (causal) window spanning W bins (ending at times t, …, t-W). Therefore, if decoding from n neurons, there were (W + 1)n predictors of the target variable in a given bin. In practice, we used W = 10. To decode these continuous-valued targets, we performed linear regression using the Lasso module from scikit-learn111 (v.1.1.2) with 0.001 tolerance, 1,000 maximum iterations and “fit_intercept” set to True. Decoding performance was evaluated using the R2 metric.\n    73→"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":"toolu_01KpMnXXVaYJy5azWgjegnBW","session_id":"82066a95-4c48-418f-82c6-11f3de05a7cb","uuid":"a161da87-5e69-468f-bb29-e70983f2a87c"}

**min_rt=0.08s, max_rt=2.0s**: Reaction time bounds (firstMovement_times - stimOn_times)\n  - **nan_exclude (default)**: ['stimOn_times', 'choice', 'feedback_times', 'probabilityLeft', 'firstMovement_times', 'feedbackType']\n  - **exclude_unbiased=False**: Removes trials with probabilityLeft == 0.5 when True\n  - **exclude_nochoice=True**: Removes trials where choice == 0\n  - **min/max_trial_len**: Filters by (feedback_times - goCue_times)\n- Returns: (trials DataFrame, boolean mask Series)\n\n#### **list_brain_regions(neural_dict, **kwargs)**\n- Converts cluster regions to Beryl mapping\n- Parameters: `single_region` kwarg determines if regions split individually or grouped\n- Returns: list of regions, beryl_reg mapping\n\n#### **select_brain_regions(regressors, beryl_reg, region, **kwargs)**\n- Filters cluster indices by brain region membership\n- Returns: reg_clu_ids array of selected cluster indices\n\n#### **create_intervals(start_time, end_time, interval_len)**\n- Creates non-overlapping time intervals\n- Returns: (n_intervals, 2) array with [start, end] pairs\n\n#### **get_spike_data_per_interval(times, clusters, interval_begs, interval_ends, interval_len, binsize, n_workers=os.cpu_count())**\n- Bins spike counts across intervals using multiprocessing\n- Key logic:\n  - `n_bins = int(np.ceil(interval_len / binsize))`\n  - Selects spikes: `(times >= t_beg) & (times < t_end)`\n  - Uses `bincount2D()` for binning with `xbin=binsize, xlim=[t_beg, t_end]`\n  - Returns array of shape (n_intervals, n_clusters, n_bins)\n\n#### **bin_spiking_data(reg_clu_ids, neural_df, intervals=None, trials_df=None, n_workers=os.cpu_count(), **kwargs)**\n- Formats session-wide spikes into trial-based arrays\n- Key kwargs:\n  - `align_time`: 'firstMovement_times' | 'stimOn_times' | 'feedback_times'\n  - `time_window`: (window_start, window_end) relative to align_time\n  - `binsize`: Bin width in seconds (optional, defaults to chunk_len)\n- Two binning modes:\n  1. If `chunk_len / binsize == 1.0`: Single bin per interval using `get_spike_counts_in_bins()`\n  2. Otherwise: Multi-bin using `get_spike_data_per_interval()`\n- Returns: (list of 2D arrays shape (n_bins, n_clusters), cluster_ids array)\n\n#### **load_target_behavior(one, eid, target)**\n- Loads behavioral signals from multiple sources\n- Supported targets:\n  - Wheel: 'wheel-position', 'wheel-velocity', 'wheel-speed'\n  - Whiskers: 'left-whisker-motion-energy', 'right-whisker-motion-energy'\n  - Pupils: 'left-pupil-diameter', 'right-pupil-diameter'\n  - Paw speeds (via DLC): 'left/right-camera-left/right-paw-speed'\n  - Nose speed: 'left/right-nose-speed'\n- Returns: dict with 'times', 'values', and optional 'skip' flag\n\n#### **get_behavior_per_interval(target_times, target_vals, intervals=None, trials_df=None, allow_nans=False, n_workers=os.cpu_count(), **kwargs)**\n- Formats session-wide behavior into interval arrays with interpolation\n- Key logic:\n  - Splits data into intervals using `np.searchsorted()`\n  - Interpolates using `scipy.interpolate.interp1d()` with linear kind and 'extrapolate' fill\n  - `x_interp = np.linspace(interval_begs + binsize, interval_ends, n_bins)` (starts at binsize offset)\n  - Skips intervals with data gaps > binsize\n- Validation checks:\n  - No empty intervals (`len(target_vals) == 0`)\n  - NaN handling (`allow_nans=False` skips intervals with any NaNs)\n  - Data coverage at interval start/end (must be within binsize)\n- Returns: (target_times_list, target_vals_list, good_interval mask, skip_reasons)\n\n#### **load_anytime_behaviors(one, eid, n_workers=os.cpu_count())**\n- Loads all 6 continuous behaviors in parallel:\n  - 'wheel-velocity', 'wheel-speed'\n  - 'left-whisker-motion-energy', 'right-whisker-motion-energy'\n  - 'left-pupil-diameter', 'right-pupil-diameter'\n- Returns: dict mapping behavior names to dicts with 'times' and 'values'\n\n#### **bin_behaviors(one, eid, behaviors, intervals=None, trials_only=False, trials_df=None, mask=None, allow_nans=True, n_workers=os.cpu_count(), **kwargs)**\n- Bins all behavior variables across intervals\n- Special handling:\n  - 'whisker-motion-energy': Tries left, falls back to right\n  - 'pupil-diameter': Tries left, falls back to right\n  - Extracts from trials: choice (binary), block (probabilityLeft), reward (rewardVolume > 1), contrast (contrastLeft - contrastRight)\n- Returns: (behave_dict with behavior arrays, mask_dict with validity masks)\n\n#### **prepare_data(one, eid, bwm_df, params, n_workers=os.cpu_count())**\n- Orchestration function: loads all data for a session\n- Steps:\n  1. Get all probe IDs for session via `one.eid2pid()`\n  2. Load spikes/clusters for each probe\n  3. Merge probes\n  4. Load trials with mask\n  5. Load all anytime behaviors\n- Returns: (neural_dict, behave_dict, meta_data, trials_data)\n\n#### **align_spike_behavior(binned_spikes, binned_behaviors, beh_names, trials_mask=None)**\n- Aligns neural and behavior trial counts\n- Removes trials where any behavior is None or trial fails quality mask\n- Returns: (aligned_binned_spikes, aligned_binned_behaviors)\n\n---\n\n## 2. DATASET UTILS (`dataset_utils.py`)\n\n### Constants\n- **DATA_COLUMNS**: ['spikes_sparse_data', 'spikes_sparse_indices', 'spikes_sparse_indptr', 'spikes_sparse_shape', 'cluster_depths']\n- **Target EIDs file**: 'data/target_eids.txt'\n- **Test RE EIDs file**: 'data/test_re_eids.txt'\n\n### Functions\n\n#### **get_sparse_from_binned_spikes(binned_spikes)**\n- Converts dense spike arrays to scipy sparse format (csr_array)\n- dtype: np.ubyte (unsigned 8-bit integer)\n- Returns: (sparse matrices, data lists, indices lists, indptr lists, shape tuples)\n\n#### **get_binned_spikes_from_sparse(spikes_sparse_data_list, spikes_sparse_indices_list, spikes_sparse_indptr_list, spikes_sparse_shape_list)**\n- Reconstructs dense arrays from sparse format\n- Returns: numpy array of binned spikes\n\n#### **create_dataset(binned_spikes, bwm_df, eid, params, meta_data=None, binned_behaviors=None)**\n- Creates HuggingFace Dataset from sparse spike data\n- Includes in dataset:\n  - Spike data (sparse format)\n  - Behavior data (if provided)\n  - Metadata (if provided):\n    - binsize, interval_len, eid, probe_name, sampling_freq\n    - cluster_regions, cluster_channels, cluster_depths\n    - good_clusters, cluster_uuids, cluster_qc\n- Returns: Dataset object\n\n#### **upload_dataset(dataset, org, eid, is_private=True)**\n- Pushes dataset to HuggingFace Hub\n\n#### **download_dataset(org, eid, split=\"train\", cache_dir=None)**\n- Loads dataset from HuggingFace Hub\n\n---\n\n## 3. DATA CACHING ORCHESTRATION (`0_data_caching.py`)\n\n### Configuration Parameters\n```python\nparams = {\n    'interval_len': 2,              # Total interval length (seconds)\n    'binsize': 0.02,                # Bin width (20 ms)\n    'single_region': False,         # Process all regions together\n    'align_time': 'stimOn_times',   # Alignment event\n    'time_window': (-.5, 1.5)       # -500ms to +1500ms around stimulus onset\n}\n```\n\n### Behavior Variables\n```python\nbeh_names = [\n    'choice', 'reward', 'block',                    # Trial-constant (from trials_df)\n    'wheel-speed', 'whisker-motion-energy',        # Time-varying (20 ms bins)\n    # 'pupil-diameter',  # Optional, excluded due to missing data in some sessions\n]\n```\n\n### Processing Pipeline\n1. **Data Loading** (`prepare_data()`):\n   - Load spikes from all probes + merge\n   - Load trials with exclusion mask\n   - Load all continuous behaviors\n\n2. **Brain Region Selection** (`list_brain_regions()`, `select_brain_regions()`):\n   - Convert to Beryl atlas mapping\n   - Select clusters in regions\n\n3. **Spike Binning** (`bin_spiking_data()`):\n   - Align to stimOn_times\n   - Extract window: -0.5s to +1.5s\n   - Bin into 0.02s (20ms) bins\n   - Total: (1.5 - (-0.5)) / 0.02 = 100 bins per trial\n\n4. **Behavior Binning** (`bin_behaviors()`):\n   - wheel-speed, whisker-motion-energy: Time-varying with 20ms bins\n   - choice, reward, block: Trial-constant values repeated across bins\n\n5. **Alignment** (`align_spike_behavior()`):\n   - Ensure spike and behavior trial counts match\n   - Remove failed trials\n\n6. **Train/Val/Test Split**:\n   - 70% train, 10% val, 20% test\n   - Randomized indices per session\n\n7. **Dataset Creation** (`create_dataset()`):\n   - Convert to sparse spike format\n   - Package with metadata\n   - Save to disk per session\n\n### Key Exclusions\n- **Trials**: min_rt=0.08s, max_rt=2.0s, max_trial_len=10.0s\n- **Default NaN exclusions**: stimOn_times, choice, feedback_times, probabilityLeft, firstMovement_times, feedbackType\n- **Sessions**: At least 400 trials (mentioned in methods.txt)\n- **Neurons**: Well-isolated only (quality criteria applied in load_spiking_data)\n\n---\n\n## 4. METHODS TEXT (`methods.txt`)\n\n### Trial-Aligned Dataset Specifications\n\n**Choice Decoding:**\n- Alignment: stimulus onset (stimOn_times)\n- Time window: -0.5s to +1.5s (2.0s total)\n- Bin size: 50ms (NOT 20ms as in code!)\n- Neurons: All spike-sorted units per session\n- Target: Binary (left/right)\n\n**Prior Decoding:**\n- Alignment: stimulus onset\n- Time window: -0.6s to -0.1s (500ms window)\n- Bin size: 50ms\n- Target: probabilityLeft (block information)\n\n**Dynamic Behaviors (wheel speed, whisker motion):**\n- Alignment: firstMovement_times (first wheel movement)\n- Time window: 0s to +1.0s after alignment\n- Bin size: 20ms (non-overlapping)\n- Sampling: 60 Hz for whisker motion energy\n- Target: Continuous values\n\n### Trial-Unaligned Dataset\n- Segments: 2-second snippets\n- Coverage: During and outside experimental trials\n- Behaviors: wheel speed, whisker motion energy only\n- No event alignment\n\n### Quality Criteria\n- Sessions: ≥250 trials, ≥90% accuracy on 100% contrast, ≥3 incorrect trials\n- Insertions: RIGOR criteria, no major artifacts, histology confirmation\n- Neurons: Amplitude > 50μV, noise cutoff < 20μV, refractory period violations excluded\n- Final: Well-isolated neurons only (75,708 out of 621,733 units)\n\n### Recording Infrastructure\n- 699 Neuropixels probes across 139 mice\n- 2 simultaneous probes typical per session\n- 12 labs (Europe and USA)\n- 270 brain regions covered\n\n---\n\n## Key Discrepancies Between Code and Methods\n\n1. **Binsize for Choice/Prior**: Methods specify 50ms, but code uses 20ms globally\n2. **Choice Alignment**: Methods specify stimulus onset (-0.5, 1.5), matches code\n3. **Prior Alignment**: Methods specify -0.6 to -0.1 window, but code appears to apply (-.5, 1.5) universally\n4. **Time Window**: Code uses time_window=(-.5, 1.5) producing 100 bins at 0.02s binsize (2.0s / 0.02s)\n\n---\n\nThis comprehensive breakdown shows the full processing pipeline: spike loading → clustering → merging probes → trial masking → behavior loading → binning (neural and behavioral) → alignment → train/val/test splitting → sparse dataset
